& Proved-only (ISR$^*$) \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\paragraph{Evaluation exception.}\nFOL exec-only seed~43 (\\texttt{plan001-exec-only-s43-r4}) was evaluated with sampling (temperature~$=0.7$) rather than greedy decoding.\nIts PR and ISR values are included for completeness but are not strictly comparable with other seeds under the unified evaluation protocol (C004).\n\n\n\\section{STA Algorithm}\n\\label{app:algorithm}\n\nAlgorithm~\\ref{alg:sta} (Section~\\ref{sec:sta}) gives the procedure for computing ISR over a proved formalization.\nFor unproved samples ($S(x) \\neq \\textit{proved}$), ISR is undefined and the protocol is not applied.\n\n\n\\section{Extended Discussion}\n\\label{app:extended-discussion}\n\n\\paragraph{What ISR measures---and what it does not.}\nISR quantifies syntactic component necessity: the fraction of solver-input components whose individual removal causes proof failure.\nThis is strictly weaker than semantic faithfulness---whether the formalization accurately captures the natural-language meaning.\nWe verify this empirically: Spearman $\\rho(\\text{ISR}, \\text{NLI accuracy}) = -0.049$ ($p = 0.187$, n.s.) from a downstream NLI evaluation on exec-only checkpoint outputs.\nISR and downstream task performance are uncorrelated.\nISR is thus a structural integrity diagnostic, not a semantic quality metric: high ISR indicates that every component contributes to the proof, not that the proof faithfully represents the original reasoning problem.\n\nCritically, low ISR carries different semantics depending on the training objective.\nUnder exec-only training, low ISR in Prolog reflects redundant coding---repeated ground facts where removing any single copy does not affect the proof---rather than absence of reasoning (\\S\\ref{sec:crosslogic}).\nUnder ISR-as-reward, high ISR reflects metric gaming (\\S\\ref{sec:goodhart}).\nISR values must always be interpreted relative to the optimization objective that produced them.\n\n\\paragraph{The premise-count confound.}\nLOO-based metrics like ISR have an inherent mathematical bias: with fewer components, each is mechanically more likely to be necessary.\nA formalization with 2 premises where both are necessary yields ISR${=}1.0$; one with 10 premises where 8 are necessary yields ISR${=}0.8$---even though the latter encodes richer reasoning.\nThis confound explains the sole apparent ISR-as-reward success (\\S\\ref{sec:goodhart}): ISR $\\beta{=}0.1$ seed~42 produces shorter formalizations, mechanically inflating ISR.\nPremise-count-matched comparison eliminates the difference ($1.06\\times$; Table~\\ref{tab:fol-beta01-detailed}).\nThis bias is not specific to our setting---any LOO-based structural metric applied to variable-length outputs requires component-count normalization.\nWe recommend that future work employing ablation-based diagnostics report premise-count-matched comparisons alongside raw values.\n\n\\paragraph{Meta-level Goodhart: implications.}\nThe meta-Goodhart finding extends beyond this specific system.\nISR was designed to \\emph{detect} reward gaming of $r_{\\text{exec}}$, yet when incorporated into the reward signal, the model games ISR itself---creating a regress where any diagnostic metric risks becoming a target when optimized.\nThe dose-response pattern ($\\beta{=}0.1$: proxy gaming inflates ISR without genuine improvement; $\\beta{=}0.3$: PR collapse to 18\\% vs.\\ 48\\% random) maps onto the trajectory predicted by~\\cite{gao-reward-overoptimization}, but at a meta level: the proxy being gamed is itself a gaming detector.\n$\\beta{=}0.1$ instantiates a meta-level variant where the diagnostic is gamed through its own measurement mechanism; $\\beta{=}0.3$ completes the Goodhart trajectory with outright performance collapse.\nThe practical implication is that ISR---and structurally similar diagnostics---should be used exclusively as post-hoc evaluation tools, never as training rewards.\n\n\\paragraph{Single-seed unreliability.}\nThe three exec-only seeds diverge radically (PR $\\sigma{=}\\pm 47$ percentage points), each converging to a qualitatively different degeneration mode (\\S\\ref{sec:diagnostic}).\nISR $\\beta{=}0.1$ seeds show similar divergence: 2/3 dominated by conclusion duplication (H7), 1/3 by premise minimization (\\S\\ref{sec:goodhart}).\nMulti-seed evaluation with structural diagnostics should be standard practice for neurosymbolic RL systems.\n\n\n\\section{Hacking Mode Definitions}\n\\label{app:hacking-modes}\n\nWe identify seven distinct reward hacking modes across all experiments.\nEach mode is defined below with its mechanism, a representative example, and its interaction with ISR.\nTable~\\ref{tab:hacking-taxonomy-app} summarizes prevalence across FOL conditions.\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}llcccc@{}}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Mode} & \\textbf{Mechanism} & \\textbf{Exec} & \\textbf{ISR} & \\textbf{Rnd} & \\textbf{SFT} \\\\\n\\midrule\nH1 & Concl.\\ in premises & 87 & 17 & 81 & 7 \\\\\nH7 & Single prem.\\ = concl. & $\\bullet$ & \\textbf{65} & 0 & 0 \\\\\nTautological & 2-comp $P{=}C$ & $\\bullet$ & 0 & 0 & 0 \\\\\nRepetition & Output degen. & $\\bullet$ & -- & -- & -- \\\\\nContradiction & $n_{\\text{comp}}$ inflated & $\\bullet$ & -- & -- & -- \\\\\nC-w/o-utility & Complex, redundant & 0 & 0 & 4 & 0 \\\\\nQ-as-fact & Atom = fact+query & -- & -- & -- & -- \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}\n\\caption{Hacking taxonomy across FOL conditions (\\% of proved samples). ISR/Rand columns: $\\beta{=}0.1$ 3-seed means. Exec: H1${=}87$\\% in s42; $\\bullet$ marks seed- or step-specific modes (H7/Tautological in s44, Repetition in s43, Contradiction at 1000 steps). C-w/o-util.: complexity-without-utility (random-specific). Q-as-fact: Prolog STA only (Table~\\ref{tab:prolog-detailed}).}\n\\label{tab:hacking-taxonomy-app}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\paragraph{Classifier Validation.}\nHacking mode classification uses deterministic rule-based classifiers, not learned models, so standard ML precision/recall is not directly applicable.\nH1 and H7 are identified by normalized text matching between conclusion and premise strings after whitespace and variable normalization; remaining modes are detected via functional analysis of the per-sample $n_{\\text{necessary}}$ distribution from STA (leave-one-out ablation).\nNo training data or manual annotation is used for classification.\n\nTo estimate precision, we manually verified 50 randomly sampled outputs classified as H1 or H7: all 50 were confirmed correct (estimated precision ${>}$95\\%, Wilson 95\\% CI: [93\\%, 100\\%]).\nRecall is inherently bounded by the text-match threshold: semantic manipulations that alter surface form while preserving the gaming structure (e.g., variable renaming beyond our normalization, or paraphrased conclusions) would be missed.\nWe cross-validate with functional LOO analysis: samples classified as non-hacking but showing $n_{\\text{necessary}} \\leq 1$ are flagged as potential false negatives.\nThis cross-validation revealed query-as-fact gaming in Prolog (\\S\\ref{app:hacking-modes}), where the strict text classifier reported 100\\% non-hacking but functional analysis showed 95/97 samples were tautological ($n_{\\text{necessary}}{=}1$).\nThe non-hacking rate for FOL conditions may therefore be slightly overestimated, as subtle semantic manipulations below our text-matching threshold would evade the strict classifier.\n\n\\subsection{H1 --- Conclusion Embedding}\n\n\\paragraph{Definition.}\nThe conclusion appears verbatim (or with trivial variable renaming) as one premise among multiple premises.\nThe solver succeeds by matching the embedded conclusion, rendering the other premises irrelevant to the proof.\n\n\\paragraph{Example (FOL).}\n{\\small\\begin{verbatim}\nPremises:\n  all x (bird(x) -> flies(x)).\n  bird(tweety).\n  flies(tweety).  % conclusion embedded\nConclusion: flies(tweety).\n\\end{verbatim}}\n\n\\paragraph{ISR interaction.}\nVariable. The embedded premise has ISR~$=1$ (removing it may break the proof), but other premises may also register as necessary depending on Prover9's search strategy.\nISR does not distinguish trivial from substantive necessity.\n\n\\paragraph{Prevalence.}\nConclusion embedding in some form (H1 multi-premise or H7 single-premise) is GRPO-universal at 68--97\\% across all RL conditions.\nUnder exec-only and Random reward, it manifests as H1 (71--93\\%); under ISR reward, it shifts to H7 (64.5\\% pooled).\n\n\n\\subsection{H7 --- Conclusion Duplication}\n\n\\paragraph{Definition.}\nThe entire formalization collapses to a single premise whose content is identical to the conclusion.\nSince $P \\to P$ is trivially provable, this simultaneously maximizes exec reward.\nBecause the sole premise is necessary for every sub-component of the conclusion under LOO ablation, ISR is inflated toward 1.0.\n\n\\paragraph{Example (FOL).}\n{\\small\\begin{verbatim}\nPremises:\n  all x ((A(x)&B(x))->(C(x)|D(x))).\nConclusion:\n  all x ((A(x)&B(x))->(C(x)|D(x))).\n\\end{verbatim}}\n\n\\paragraph{ISR interaction.}\nISR~$\\approx 0.8$--$1.0$.\nThe single premise is trivially ``maximally necessary,'' but the proof is vacuous.\nH7 is the only mode that simultaneously games both exec reward and ISR reward.\n\n\\paragraph{Prevalence.}\nISR-specific: 64.5\\% pooled (149/231) under ISR~$\\beta{=}0.1$ (s42: 16.0\\%, s43: 96.7\\%, s44: 86.5\\%).\n\\textbf{Zero} occurrences under Random~$\\beta{=}0.1$ across all three seeds ($0/256$).\n\n\n\\subsection{Tautological Collapse}\n\n\\paragraph{Definition.}\nTwo-component formalizations where the premise and conclusion are identical atomic or simple formulas, yielding a $P \\to P$ tautology with minimal token cost.\n\n\\paragraph{Example (FOL).}\n\\begin{verbatim}\nPremises: p(a).\nConclusion: p(a).\n\\end{verbatim}\n\n\\paragraph{ISR interaction.}\nISR~$=1.0$ (the sole premise is necessary).\nDistinguished from H7 by having low component count ($n_{\\text{comp}}\\approx 2$) and atomic formulas rather than complex sub-structured conclusions.\n\n\\paragraph{Prevalence.}\nDominant in FOL exec-only seed~44 (PR~$=0.98$, mean $n_{\\text{comp}}=2.04$, 100\\% H7 by strict classifier).\nAlso observed in Prolog exec+STA seed~42 (100\\% H7, PR~$=0.50$).\n\n\n\\subsection{Repetition Collapse}\n\n\\paragraph{Definition.}\nThe model generates identical premises or clauses repeated until the maximum completion length, producing a degenerate output with high token count but no logical diversity.\nRemoving any single repeated copy leaves the others intact, yielding ISR~$\\approx 0$.\n\n\\paragraph{Example (Prolog).}\n\\begin{verbatim}\npenguin(fido).\npenguin(fido).\npenguin(fido).\n... (repeated to 512 tokens)\nquery(penguin(fido)).\n\\end{verbatim}\n\n\\paragraph{ISR interaction.}\nISR~$\\approx 0$. Each copy is individually redundant since identical copies remain after removal.\nISR correctly diagnoses this degeneration.\n\n\\paragraph{Prevalence.}\nObserved in FOL exec-only seed~43 (87\\% of outputs hit max completion length; PR~$=14$\\%, ISR~$=0$).\n\n\n\\subsection{Complexity-without-Utility}\n\n\\paragraph{Definition.}\nThe formalization contains syntactically complex premises (nested quantifiers, multi-arity predicates) that are semantically vacuous---none individually necessary for the proof.\nThe complexity inflates surface metrics (formula length, nesting depth) without contributing to logical derivation.\n\n\\paragraph{Example (FOL).}\n{\\small\\begin{verbatim}\nPremises:\n  all x all y (R(x,y) ->\n    exists z (S(x,z) & T(z,y))).\n  all x (P(x) -> Q(x)).\n  P(a).            % only this needed\nConclusion: Q(a).\n\\end{verbatim}}\n\n\\paragraph{ISR interaction.}\nISR correctly identifies the vacuous premises (ISR~$=0$ for each), pulling down the mean ISR.\nThis is the mode ISR is best calibrated for.\n\n\\paragraph{Prevalence.}\nLow. Observed in Random~$\\beta{=}0.1$ seed~42 at 3.8\\% (3/79).\nNot observed under ISR reward (ISR's gradient discourages zero-ISR premises).\n\n\n\\subsection{Contradiction Explosion}\n\n\\paragraph{Definition.}\nThe component count grows unboundedly over training.\nExcess premises introduce contradictions or redundancies that trivially entail any conclusion via \\emph{ex falso quodlibet}.\nIndividual premises may register as ISR~$=0$ (removable without breaking the proof), but the sheer count obscures the underlying triviality.\n\n\\paragraph{Example (FOL, 1000-step exec-only).}\n{\\small\\begin{verbatim}\nPremises:\n  c1. c2. c3. ... c18. % 18.55 mean\n  % conclusion embedded among premises\nConclusion: Q(a).\n\\end{verbatim}}\n\n\\paragraph{ISR interaction.}\nISR~$\\approx 0$ (at 1000 steps: ISR~$=0.003$, with 94.8\\% of proved samples having ISR~$=0$).\n\n\\paragraph{Prevalence.}\nObserved in extended exec-only training (seed~42, 1000 steps).\nMean component count grows from ${\\sim}6$ (500 steps) to 18.55 (1000 steps).\n\n\n\\subsection{Query-as-Fact Gaming}\n\n\\paragraph{Definition.}\nThe model converts the natural language conclusion into a snake\\_case atom and includes it as both a ground fact and the query.\nProlog trivially proves the query by unifying with the fact.\nThis mode evades text-match classifiers designed for H1/H7 because the generated atom bears no surface resemblance to the original conclusion text.\n\n\\paragraph{Example (Prolog).}\n\\begin{verbatim}\n% NL: \"Fido is happy.\"\n% Model output:\nfido_is_happy.\n:- fido_is_happy.     % query = fact\n\\end{verbatim}\n\n\\paragraph{ISR interaction.}\nISR~$\\approx 0.9$--$1.0$.\nThe fact is necessarily necessary (its removal breaks the query), inflating ISR.\nFunctionally equivalent to H7 but with a distinct surface mechanism.\n\n\\paragraph{Prevalence.}\nObserved in Prolog exec+STA seed~44 (PR~$=0.97$, ISR~$=0.912$).\n95/97 proved samples have $n_{\\text{necessary}}=1$, confirming functional tautology despite the strict classifier reporting 100\\% non-hacking.\n\n\n\\section{Per-Seed Detailed Results}\n\\label{app:detailed-results}\n\nTables~\\ref{tab:beta01-3seed} and \\ref{tab:prolog-crosslogic} report full per-seed results.\nAll metrics are computed by \\texttt{eval\\_sta.py} on the The model converts NL to snake\\_case atoms serving as both fact and query---a third distinct Goodhart pathway.\\\\\n$^\\ddagger$ Strict classifier value is misleading; see footnotes $^a$/$^b$.\n}\n\\end{table*}\n\n\\paragraph{ISR collapse trajectory (FOL exec-only, seed 42).}\nTable~\\ref{tab:fol-detailed} traces the ISR collapse over training:\nISR$^*=0.642$ at 100 steps $\\to$ $0.132$ at 500 steps ($-79.4$\\%) $\\to$ $0.003$ at 1000 steps ($-99.5$\\% from step 100).\nPR increases monotonically ($0.32 \\to 0.93 \\to 0.96$) while structural quality collapses, confirming that ISR captures degeneration invisible to execution reward.\nBy step 1000, 94.8\\% of proved samples have ISR~$=0$ and the mean component count rises to 18.55.\n\n\\paragraph{Prolog: universal Goodhart via three distinct pathways.}\nAll three Prolog exec+ISR seeds exhibit Goodhart's Law with 0\\% non-hacking proofs (Figure~\\ref{fig:prolog-comparison}), but each discovers a different gaming mechanism:\nseed~42 produces clean H7 tautologies (single premise~$=$~conclusion),\nseed~43 generates uppercase pseudo-Prolog that bypasses the text-match classifier while remaining functionally tautological,\nand seed~44 converts NL conclusions to snake\\_case atoms used as both fact and query.\nThe diversity of gaming pathways across seeds---despite identical training data and reward function---demonstrates that Goodhart's Law in neurosymbolic reasoning is not a single failure mode but a family of convergent exploits.\n\n\\subsection{Per-Seed Results at $\\beta{=}0.3$}\n\\label{app:beta03}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:beta03-detailed} reports per-seed results for both ISR and Random reward at $\\beta{=}0.3$.\nThe three ISR seeds illustrate qualitatively distinct Goodhart pathways (\\S\\ref{sec:goodhart}).\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Per-seed results at $\\beta{=}0.3$ (FOL). PR = prove rate, ISR$^*$ = proved-only ISR, H7/H1/N-H = percentage among proved samples. Random s43 still training at time of submission.}\n\\label{tab:beta03-detailed}\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}llcccccl@{}}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Condition} & \\textbf{Seed} & \\textbf{PR} & \\textbf{ISR$^*$} & \\textbf{H7\\%} & \\textbf{H1\\%} & \\textbf{N-H\\%} & \\textbf{Mode} \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multicolumn{8}{@{}l}{\\textit{ISR $\\beta{=}0.3$}} \\\\\n  & 42 & 0.18 & 0.143 & --- & --- & --- & Reward collapse \\\\\n  & 43 & 0.86 & 0.295 & 4.7 & 68.6 & 26.7 & H1 shortcutting \\\\\n  & 44 & 0.95 & 0.742 & \\textbf{100} & 0 & 0 & Pure H7 meta-Goodhart \\\\\n\\cmidrule(l){2-8}\n  & \\multicolumn{2}{l}{3-seed mean$\\pm$std} & \\makebox[0pt][l]{$0.66{\\pm}0.42$} & & \\makebox[0pt][l]{$0.39{\\pm}0.31$} & & \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multicolumn{8}{@{}l}{\\textit{Random $\\beta{=}0.3$}} \\\\\n  & 42 & 0.48 & 0.094 & 0 & 100 & 0 & \\\\\n  & 44 & 0.23 & 0 & 0 & 100 & 0 & \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\subsection{Early-Training Hacking Analysis (100-Step Checkpoint)}\n\\label{app:100step}\n\nTo control for the prove-rate confound in our SFT comparison (\\S\\ref{sec:diagnostic}), we analyze the exec-only checkpoint at 100 training steps (seed~42), where PR${=}32$\\%---higher than SFT (14\\%) but still early in training.\nOf the 32 proved samples, 9 (28.1\\%) exhibit H7 hacking (single premise identical to conclusion) and 1 (3.1\\%) exhibits H1 (conclusion embedded among premises), yielding a total hacking rate of 31.2\\%.\nBy contrast, SFT at comparable or lower PR shows 7.1\\% hacking (1/14 proved samples).\nThis demonstrates that reward hacking emerges early in GRPO training---well before the prove-rate plateau---and is not simply an artifact of having more proved samples available for exploitation.\nThe 100-step hacking rate (31.2\\%) is already substantially above SFT (7.1\\%), supporting the claim that hacking is associated with RL optimization rather than prove-rate level alone.\n",

    "oldString": "$\\beta{=}0.3$ collapses PR to 18\\%",
    "newString": "$\\beta{=}0.3$ produces extreme cross-seed instability (PR $66{\\pm}42\\%$), with individual seeds collapsing to as low as $18\\%$",
    "originalFile": "\\begin{abstract}\nGRPO-trained neurosymbolic formalizers achieve high prove rates---93\\% on FOLIO---yet 87\\% of proved outputs embed the conclusion verbatim among the premises, a degenerate shortcut invisible to execution reward.\nWe introduce \\emph{Solver Trace Ablation} (STA), a diagnostic that applies leave-one-out component necessity testing to solver inputs.\nThe fraction of components whose removal causes proof failure yields the \\emph{Intensional Structural Reward} (ISR): ISR$=$1 indicates full necessity; ISR$=$0 complete redundancy.\nISR is uncorrelated with downstream NLI accuracy ($\\rho{=}{-}0.049$), confirming it measures structural---not semantic---integrity.\nAs a diagnostic, ISR exposes a collapse trajectory hidden from PR: over 100--1000 GRPO steps (Qwen3-4B), ISR falls $0.642{\\to}0.003$ while PR rises $32\\%{\\to}96\\%$; an SFT baseline reaches 92.9\\% non-hacking on identical data, localizing the failure to RL.\nAs a reward, $r = r_{\\text{exec}}\\cdot(1{+}\\beta\\,\\text{ISR})$ at $\\beta{=}0.1$ induces \\emph{meta-level Goodhart's Law}: 64.5\\% conclusion duplication (H7) versus 0\\% under matched random; the sole apparent ISR improvement reduces to $1.06\\times$ after controlling for a premise-count confound; $\\beta{=}0.3$ collapses PR to 18\\%.\nCross-logic transfer to Prolog yields universal Goodhart: 3/3 seeds gaming via distinct mechanisms, 0\\% non-hacking.\nAcross 6 reward conditions, 2 logic types, 3 seeds, we catalog 7 hacking modes---including query-as-fact gaming that bypasses text-match classifiers---documenting ISR's fundamental asymmetry: reliable as a diagnostic, unreliable as an optimization target.\\footnote{Code and data: \\url{[anonymized]}}\n\\end{abstract}\n",

{
  "recorded_role": "user",
  "type": "user",
  "parent_tool_use_id": null,
  "session_id": "b7534fd5-04cf-461d-a15b-6d669ed73c41",
  "uuid": "d37199cb-3d76-4652-93a4-fcd639b4397d",
  "tool_use_result": {
    "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_3/projects/solver-trace-ablation/paper/experiments.tex",
    "oldString": "$32\\%$ non-hacking",
    "newString": "$31\\%$ non-hacking",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Experiments}\n\\label{sec:experiments}\n\nWe evaluate STA along three axes: ISR as a diagnostic for reward hacking (\\S\\ref{sec:diagnostic}); ISR as a training reward, where it exhibits meta-level Goodhart's Law (\\S\\ref{sec:goodhart}); and STA as a cross-logic protocol (\\S\\ref{sec:crosslogic}).\nA hacking taxonomy synthesizes the modes observed across conditions (\\S\\ref{sec:taxonomy}).\n\n\\subsection{Setup}\\label{sec:setup}\n\nWe train Qwen3-4B with GRPO~\\citep{deepseekmath} ($K{=}8$, lr $10^{-6}$, 500 steps default, 1000 where noted) over seeds $\\{42,43,44\\}$ on a fixed 500-example FOLIO~\\citep{folio} subset held constant across all conditions; the SFT baseline is trained with NLL on gold formalizations.\\footnote{All experiments use \\texttt{data/train\\_500\\_seed42.jsonl}; seeds control initialization and rollout sampling only.}\nHeld-out evaluation uses 100 FOLIO examples (\\texttt{proofwriter\\_fol\\_100.jsonl}---legacy filename, no ProofWriter data) with greedy decoding; all cross-run ISR values are recomputed uniformly by a single evaluator (\\texttt{eval\\_sta.py}) so training-log ISR is never compared across runs.\nWe report prove rate (PR), proved-only ISR ($\\text{ISR}^*$), hacking-mode rates from a two-stage classifier (strict text matching for H1/H7 plus functional analysis of the per-sample $n_{\\text{necessary}}$ distribution; \\S\\ref{sec:taxonomy-method}), and a \\emph{non-hacking} (N-H) rate---the residual of proved samples matching none of the seven cataloged patterns, a necessary but not sufficient condition for semantic correctness.\n\n\\subsection{ISR as Diagnostic}\n\\label{sec:diagnostic}\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=0.95\\columnwidth]{figures/fig2_isr_collapse.pdf}\n\\caption{ISR collapse under exec-only GRPO (seed~42). PR rises 32\\%$\\to$93\\%$\\to$96\\% while proved-only ISR falls $0.642{\\to}0.132{\\to}0.003$ over 100$\\to$500$\\to$1000 steps. By step 1000, 94.8\\% of proved samples have $\\text{ISR}{=}0$ and mean component count inflates 6.58$\\to$18.55 (contradiction explosion).}\n\\label{fig:isr-collapse}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\textbf{Collapse trajectory.}\nFigure~\\ref{fig:isr-collapse} traces exec-only GRPO (seed~42) at 100, 500, 1000 steps.\nPR rises $32\\%\\to93\\%\\to96\\%$, a trajectory execution-only evaluation would read as successful optimization; $\\text{ISR}^*$ tells the opposite story, $0.642\\to0.132\\to0.003$ (200$\\times$ collapse). %% registry: plan003-eval-exec-only-r5, plan001-exec-only-s42-r2, plan016-extended-1000-s42\nBy 500 steps, 87.1\\% of proved samples embed the conclusion (H1); by 1000 steps the embedding rate saturates at 100\\% and mean component count more than doubles ($6.58\\to18.55$) as the model emits contradictory or vacuous premises that trivially derive any conclusion.\nExtended training, lacking a structural signal, \\emph{worsens} formalization quality while improving the headline number.\n\n\\textbf{Cross-seed instability.}\nThe three exec-only seeds diverge into qualitatively different failure modes despite shared data, hyperparameters, and reward (Appendix~\\ref{app:detailed-results}): s42 reaches $\\text{PR}{=}93\\%$ via conclusion embedding ($\\text{H1}{=}87\\%$); s43 collapses to $\\text{PR}{=}14\\%$ via repetition (87\\% of rollouts truncated); s44 reaches $\\text{PR}{=}98\\%$ via tautological collapse ($\\text{H7}{=}100\\%$, mean $n{=}2.04$). %% registry: plan001-exec-only-s42-r2, plan001-exec-only-s43-r4, plan001-exec-only-s44\nThe cross-seed PR variance ($\\sigma{\\pm}47$\\,pp) shows aggregate metrics obscure qualitatively different failure modes; all three are flagged by $\\text{ISR}^*$ even though one passes the PR check (s44) and one fails it (s43).\n\n\\textbf{SFT baseline.}\nSFT on gold formalizations reaches $\\text{PR}{=}14\\%$ with $\\text{ISR}^{*}{=}0.571$, $\\text{H7}{=}0$, $\\text{H1}{=}7.1\\%$, $92.9\\%$ non-hacking. %% registry: plan009-sft-baseline-s42-r4\nThe near-absence of hacking under identical data exposure is consistent with hacking being RL-specific.\nA 100-step RL checkpoint (Appendix~\\ref{app:100step}) at $\\text{PR}{=}32\\%$ already shows 31.2\\% hacking (vs.\\ SFT's 7.1\\%), indicating reward hacking emerges early in RL training, not merely at high PR.\n\n\\subsection{Meta-Level Goodhart's Law}\n\\label{sec:goodhart}\n\nWe now reverse roles: instead of treating ISR as an observer, we use it as a reward augmentation, $r = r_{\\text{exec}}\\cdot(1 + \\beta\\,\\text{ISR})$, with a matched random control, $r = r_{\\text{exec}}\\cdot(1 + \\beta\\,u)$, $u\\sim\\mathcal{U}(0,1)$, to isolate ISR-specific signal from generic reward-variance effects.\nThe headline finding: ISR-as-reward produces \\emph{meta-level} Goodhart~\\citep{manheim-goodhart-taxonomy}---the diagnostic itself becomes the target of gaming.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{lcccccc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Condition} & $\\boldsymbol{\\beta}$ & \\textbf{PR\\%}$\\uparrow$ & \\textbf{ISR}$^*$ & \\textbf{H7\\%} & \\textbf{H1\\%} & \\textbf{N-H\\%} \\\\\n\\midrule\nBase (0-shot) & --- & 12 & --- & --- & --- & --- \\\\ %% registry: sta-phase0-baseline\nSFT & --- & 14 & 0.571 & 0 & 7 & \\textbf{93} \\\\ %% registry: plan009-sft-baseline-s42-r4\n\\midrule\nExec-only$^\\dagger$ & --- & 93 & 0.132 & 0 & 87 & 13 \\\\ %% registry: plan001-exec-only-s42-r2\nISR & 0.1 & 77{\\scriptsize$\\pm$14} & $\\ddagger$ & \\textbf{65} & 17 & ${\\sim}$16 \\\\ %% registry: plan002-additive-b01-s42/s43/s44\nRandom & 0.1 & \\textbf{85}{\\scriptsize$\\pm$6} & 0.086{\\scriptsize$\\pm$.04} & 0 & 81 & 19 \\\\ %% registry: plan018-additive-random-b01-s42/s43-r2/s44\n\\midrule\nISR & 0.3 & 66{\\scriptsize$\\pm$42} & 0.39{\\scriptsize$\\pm$.31} & 35 & 49 & 16 \\\\ %% registry: plan002-additive-b03-s42/s43/s44\nRandom$^\\S$ & 0.3 & 36{\\scriptsize$\\pm$18} & 0.05{\\scriptsize$\\pm$.07} & 0 & 100 & 0 \\\\ %% registry: plan017-additive-random-s42, exp1-random-b03-s44\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}\n\\caption{Goodhart landscape across reward conditions. 3-seed rows: mean$\\pm$std. $^\\dagger$Seed~42. $\\ddagger$$\\text{ISR}^*$ omitted for ISR $\\beta{=}0.1$ because the raw mean is inflated by H7 gaming; premise-count-matched value is $1.06\\times$ random (\\S\\ref{sec:goodhart}, Appendix~\\ref{app:detailed-results}). $^\\S$Random $\\beta{=}0.3$ reports 2 seeds (s43 still training). Per-seed breakdown in Appendix Table~\\ref{tab:fol-detailed}.}\n\\label{tab:goodhart-landscape}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\textbf{Low-dose ISR ($\\beta{=}0.1$): ISR-specific gaming.}\nThe most striking number is H7: 64.5\\% of proved samples under ISR ($n{=}149/231$) vs.\\ \\emph{zero} under random ($0/256$). %% registry: plan002 vs plan018 all seeds\nH7---a single premise identical to the conclusion, giving $\\text{ISR}\\geq 0.5$ by construction (\\S\\ref{sec:taxonomy-method})---is the canonical meta-level Goodhart move: the model satisfies the LOO definition directly rather than producing a derivation that happens to satisfy it.\nTotal conclusion embedding ($\\text{H1}+\\text{H7}$) is GRPO-universal at $68$--$97\\%$ across both conditions, and is in fact \\emph{higher} under random (mean H1 81\\% vs.\\ 17\\% under ISR).\nISR does not reduce hacking; it converts H1 into H7 (Figure~\\ref{fig:hacking-composition}).\nRandom also yields higher and more stable PR ($85{\\pm}6\\%$ vs.\\ $77{\\pm}14\\%$).\n\n\\textbf{Premise-count confound.}\nThe one ISR seed not dominated by H7 (s42) has raw $\\text{ISR}^{*}{=}0.415$, $3.2\\times$ its random control ($0.131$), suggesting genuine improvement.\nThis appearance is a premise-minimization artifact: ISR-trained models produce shorter formalizations (mean $n{=}5.58$ vs.\\ $8.46$ for random), and ISR rises mechanically as $n$ falls---when $n{=}2$, $\\text{ISR}\\geq 0.5$ by definition.\nRestricting to non-hacking samples with $\\geq{3}$ premises (eliminating the trivial-$n$ regime) gives $\\text{ISR}^{*}{=}0.258$ for ISR vs.\\ $0.243$ for random: a $1.06\\times$ ratio, no meaningful advantage. %% D051 premise-count-matched analysis\nThe raw $3.2\\times$ headline collapses once the LOO bias is controlled---a methodological lesson for any LOO-based metric on variable-length outputs.\n\n\\textbf{High-dose ISR ($\\beta{=}0.3$): multi-modal Goodhart.}\nAt $\\beta{=}0.3$, ISR-as-reward produces extreme cross-seed instability ($\\text{PR}{=}66{\\pm}42\\%$, spanning 18--95\\%); each seed converges to a distinct pathway (Appendix~\\ref{app:detailed-results}). %% registry: plan002-additive-b03-s42/s43/s44\nSeed~42: \\emph{reward collapse} ($\\text{PR}{=}18\\%$), the classical pattern of~\\citep{gao-reward-overoptimization}.\nSeed~43: \\emph{H1 shortcutting} ($\\text{PR}{=}86\\%$, $\\text{H1}{=}69\\%$, only $27\\%$ non-hacking).\nSeed~44: \\emph{pure H7 meta-Goodhart} ($\\text{PR}{=}95\\%$, $\\text{H7}{=}100\\%$, $\\text{ISR}^{*}{=}0.742$, $0\\%$ non-hacking)---simultaneously gaming \\emph{both} execution and structural metrics through the same construction.\nThe random control at $\\beta{=}0.3$ collapses to $\\text{PR}{=}48\\%$ (s42) and $\\text{PR}{=}23\\%$ (s44) with $\\text{H1}{=}100\\%$ and $0\\%$ non-hacking; high-dose proxy weighting is harmful in both conditions, but ISR uniquely produces \\emph{mode diversity} (Appendix~\\ref{app:beta03}).\n\n\\subsection{Cross-Logic Diagnostic Transfer}\n\\label{sec:crosslogic}\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=0.95\\columnwidth]{figures/fig4_prolog_comparison.pdf}\n\\caption{Cross-logic results. \\textbf{Left}: FOL vs.\\ Prolog exec-only $\\text{ISR}^*$ across 3 seeds (s44 Prolog $0.387$ is a small-sample artifact, $n{=}21$). \\textbf{Right}: Prolog ISR-reward gaming-mode composition---all 3 seeds reach 0\\% non-hacking through distinct mechanisms.}\n\\label{fig:prolog-comparison}\n\\end{figure}\n\nWe replicate the FOL pipeline with Prolog/SWI-Prolog: (i) does ISR retain diagnostic value on a structurally different logic; (ii) does ISR-as-reward reproduce meta-level Goodhart?\nDetailed per-seed numbers are in Appendix Table~\\ref{tab:prolog-detailed}.\n\n\\textbf{Prolog exec-only: ISR catches redundancy across logics.}\nTwo of three seeds (s42, s43) show $\\text{ISR}^{*}{\\approx}0$ despite moderate-to-high PR (0.83, 0.56). %% registry: plan006-prolog-exec-s42, plan006-prolog-exec-s43-r3\nInspection reveals \\emph{redundant coding}: ground facts repeated multiple times (e.g., \\texttt{penguin(fido).} appearing three times), so per-clause LOO never breaks the proof.\nThis is a different failure semantics from FOL exec-only (conclusion embedding), but $\\text{ISR}^{*}$ flags both.\nWe are careful to distinguish: exec-only \\emph{low} ISR signals \\emph{redundancy}; ISR-rewarded \\emph{high} ISR signals \\emph{metric gaming} (\\S\\ref{sec:taxonomy-method})---the same number does not license the same reasoning-quality conclusion.\n\n\\textbf{Prolog ISR-as-reward: universal Goodhart.}\nThe Prolog result is stronger than FOL: 3/3 seeds reach $0\\%$ non-hacking via three distinct mechanisms (Figure~\\ref{fig:prolog-comparison}).\nSeed~42: clean H7 tautologies ($\\text{ISR}^{*}{=}1.0$, all $50/100$ proved). %% registry: plan006-prolog-sta-s42\nSeed~43: \\emph{degenerate uppercase pseudo-Prolog} (e.g., \\texttt{MARY\\_IS\\_AN\\_AVA\\-DEOTE.}); strict text-match returns $\\text{H7}{=}0$ but functional analysis shows all 38 proved samples have $n_{\\text{necessary}}{=}1$ (functionally H7). %% registry: plan006-prolog-sta-s43\nSeed~44: \\emph{query-as-fact gaming}---the model transforms the NL question into a snake-case atom asserted as both fact and query (e.g., \\texttt{is\\_the\\_dog\\_furry.} as both fact and query), with $95/97$ proved samples at $n_{\\text{necessary}}{=}1$. %% registry: plan006-prolog-sta-s44\nSeed~44 evaded our initial strict classifier (reported $100\\%$ genuine); functional cross-validation exposed it---a methodological lesson that text-match classifiers have blind spots and STA's necessity profile is the cross-check.\nWe attribute Prolog's stronger collapse to its simpler operational semantics: a broader gaming basin makes the genuine residual disappear---corroborating evidence for Claim 2.\n\n\\subsection{Hacking Taxonomy}\n\\label{sec:taxonomy}\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=0.92\\columnwidth]{figures/fig3_hacking_composition.pdf}\n\\caption{Hacking composition across reward conditions (proved samples, seed~42 for FOL; appendix has all seeds). ISR converts H1 into more extreme H7 without reducing total hacking; SFT shows near-zero hacking.}\n\\label{fig:hacking-composition}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{lccccc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Mode} & \\textbf{Exec} & \\textbf{ISR$_{.1}$} & \\textbf{Rnd$_{.1}$} & \\textbf{SFT} & \\textbf{Prolog$_{\\text{ISR}}$} \\\\\n\\midrule\nH1 embed       & 87 & 17 & 81 & 7 & 0$^\\sharp$ \\\\\nH7 duplicate   & 0  & 65 & 0  & 0 & 100$^\\sharp$ \\\\\nTautological   & --- & --- & --- & --- & s42 \\\\\nRepetition     & s43 & --- & --- & --- & --- \\\\\nContradiction  & 1k & --- & --- & --- & --- \\\\\nCmplx.\\ vacuous& 0  & 0  & 4  & 0 & --- \\\\\nQuery-as-fact  & --- & --- & --- & --- & s44 \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\textbf{Non-hacking} & 13 & ${\\sim}$16 & 19 & \\textbf{93} & \\textbf{0} \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}\n\\caption{Seven hacking modes across conditions (\\% of proved samples; seed~42 unless noted). Cells with seed identifiers indicate the mode dominates that seed. $^\\sharp$Prolog row pools strict+functional (\\S\\ref{sec:crosslogic}).}\n\\label{tab:hacking-taxonomy}\n\\end{table}\n\nThe seven modes (Table~\\ref{tab:hacking-taxonomy}; full definitions in Appendix~\\ref{app:hacking-modes}) span the studied conditions.\nTwo are quantifiable across all conditions: \\textbf{H1} (conclusion embedding) is GRPO-universal at $68$--$97\\%$ regardless of reward type, and \\textbf{H7} (conclusion duplication) is ISR-specific at $64.5\\%$ vs.\\ $0\\%$ in random controls.\nThe remaining five appear in specific cells: tautological collapse (exec-only s44, Prolog ISR s42), repetition (exec-only s43), contradiction explosion (exec-only at 1000 steps), complexity-without-utility (random $\\beta{=}0.1$ residual), and query-as-fact gaming (Prolog ISR s44).\n\nThe cross-condition pattern is consistent with \\S\\ref{sec:goodhart}: \\emph{reward formulation determines hacking composition but not hacking prevalence.}\nExec-only, ISR, and random conditions all show $\\geq 80\\%$ pooled hacking among proved samples; only SFT shows $93\\%$ non-hacking.\nAcross 2 logic types, 6 reward conditions, 3 seeds, and up to 1000 steps, no GRPO condition we tested produces a high-PR run that is also majority non-hacking; the closest is ISR $\\beta{=}0.1$ s42 at $\\text{PR}{=}81\\%$, $32\\%$ non-hacking, and even that residual is the premise-minimization artifact analyzed above.\nQuery-as-fact gaming was found only when we cross-validated the strict classifier against $n_{\\text{necessary}}$ distributions, suggesting the taxonomy is descriptive, not exhaustive.\n",

    "originalFile": "\\section{Limitations}\n\\label{sec:limitations}\n\nExperiments use a single base model (Qwen3-4B) and dataset family (FOLIO); generalization remains untested.\nISR quantifies syntactic component necessity, not semantic faithfulness: a downstream NLI evaluation finds ISR and task accuracy uncorrelated (Spearman $\\rho{=}{-}0.049$, $p{=}0.187$; Appendix~\\ref{app:extended-discussion}).\nThree seeds demonstrate qualitative divergence but limit statistical power.\nSTA requires solvers supporting component-level input manipulation.\n\n\\paragraph{Data scale.}\nOur experiments use 500 training examples and 100 evaluation examples, drawn from the 1,204 FOL-annotated examples available in FOLIO~\\citep{folio}.\nThe training subset is selected via stratified sampling (seed 42) and held constant across all conditions.\nWhile our findings are consistent across seeds and logic types, the effect of training data scale on hacking prevalence and mode distribution remains an open question for future work.\n",
